    the archives de la guerre. It prints Napoleon's and the marshals' letters, the 2nd and 5th
    Bulletins and a later one, General Suchet's and Marshal Davout's reports, the 3rd corps's
    journal, Foucher's report on the 5th corps's artillery and M. de Mumpfling's Saxon report in
    French translation. Foucart adds notes quoting Colonel Blein and Lieutenant-Colonel von der
    Goltz, and his own tables.
  - Höpfner's *Der Krieg von 1806 und 1807*, Theil 1, Bd. 1 (1850), the Prussian history from the
    war archive, with reflections on each battle.

  92 records were registered: two OCR parents, two selections, and 44 page images (26 of Foucart,
  18 of Höpfner) with their figure transcriptions. Every figure in a strength value or a recorded
  loss is read from a page image. Sums are labelled as ours.
  - Bodart matches these sources in places, by our arithmetic:
    - At Saalfeld his 14 French guns equal Foucher's 2, 10 and 2, and his 33 guns Höpfner's 15
      and 18.
    - At Jena his side B officers (312, with 9 generals and 46 staff officers) and his Saxon
      officers (114) equal Höpfner's.
    - At Auerstedt his 3,000 prisoners and 115 cannon equal the 3rd corps's journal, and so do
      his 252 French officers.

    The passages read do not show what he used.
  - Mumpfling's report is a Saxon witness. It is counted in Foucart's family because it is printed
    in the same edition.
- **Drafts.** 65 claims with 1,220 citations, all supported. Every draft covers the nine
  dimensions.
  - Phases: 29 unresolved, 13 commander-created, 12 post-outcome and 11 inherited.
  - Posture:
    - Saalfeld: the accounts differ, so there are two claims, both commander-created. In Lannes's
      letter, the 2nd Bulletin and Höpfner, Lannes attacked the prince's position and the
      prince's last advance was a show of attack (A_attacked). In Suchet's report and Mumpfling's
      the prince made a main attack of his own (both_attacked).
    - Jena: both sides attacked (both_attacked, commander-created). The French attacked
      Tauentzien's detachment and later Hohenlohe's line. Hohenlohe, Holtzendorf and Rüchel made
      main attacks of their own.
    - Auerstedt: a meeting engagement in which both sides attacked (both_attacked,
      commander-created).
  - Fortification: none_named for all three, tagged unresolved.
    - Saalfeld: a coding that counted the barricaded gates would give B_fortified. One that also
      counted the garden walls covering the French skirmishers would give both_fortified.
    - Jena: counting the garden enclosures of Vierzehnheiligen as walls would give A_fortified.
    - Auerstedt: Hassenhausen was held from its houses, hollow roads, hedges and gardens, with no
      works named.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - Saalfeld:
    - Side A's strength: 12,000 with 14 guns (Bodart); hardly more than 4,000 of the division
      Suchet engaged (Lannes); half of that division (the 2nd Bulletin); about 14,000 (Höpfner).
    - The guns taken: 21 with 6 more expected, then 36 to 40 (Lannes); 25 guns and 2 howitzers
      (Foucher); 30 (the Bulletin); 33 (the parenthesis in Suchet's report, Höpfner and Bodart).
    - The prisoners: from about 800 (Lannes) to 1,500 or 1,800 (the parentheses in Suchet's
      report), against Bodart's 1,100.
    - The French loss: 172 for Suchet's division (Suchet); about 200 with 14 officers (Bodart).
    - The prince's last advance: a show of attack (Höpfner); a charge to carry the heights
      (Suchet); a decisive attack (Mumpfling).
    - Whether Hohenlohe's order reached the prince.
  - Jena:
    - Side A's strength: 96,000, of whom 54,000 came into action (Bodart). Foucart's tables give
      83,600 men and 12,300 horses, 95,900 in all (our arithmetic), with several divisions present
      but not engaged.
    - Side B's strength: 54,000 with 17,000 Saxons (Bodart); some 50,000 (Höpfner); 40,000 for
      Hohenlohe and 15,000 for Rüchel (Goltz); 44,000 and 28,000 (Foucart's estimates); 80,000
      for the two corps before Jena (the 5th Bulletin).
    - The French loss:
      - 1,000 or 1,100 killed and 3,000 wounded (the 5th Bulletin), read as covering both fields;
      - 2,645 with 75 officers for Suchet's division, perhaps with Saalfeld (Suchet);
      - about 6,000 with 333 officers (Bodart);
      - no credible figure (Höpfner).
    - Side B's loss:
      - 30,000 to 40,000 prisoners and more than 20,000 killed or wounded for both fields (the
        Bulletin);
      - about 12,000 killed and wounded, 15,000 prisoners and 112 cannon (Bodart, the men from
        French sources);
      - 312 officers (our arithmetic) and 24 Prussian guns without the battalion guns, the men's
        loss not to be stated (Höpfner).
    - The French centre's second attack: at Hohlstedt (the Bulletin); at Vierzehnheiligen
      (Foucart's note, from Suchet's report).
  - Auerstedt:
    - Side A's strength: 27,300 with 44 guns (Bodart); 26,000 present of 28,874 effective
      (Foucart); about 33000 (Höpfner).
    - Side B's strength: 50,000 (Bodart, Goltz, the 5th Bulletin and Höpfner); about 48—49,000
      (Höpfner's reckoning); 54,000 (Foucart's estimate); more than 60,000 (Davout).
    - The French loss: 252 officers and 6,581 men in three divisions (the 3rd corps's journal);
      7,100 with 252 officers (Bodart); 270 officers and 7000 men (a French self-report in
      Höpfner).
    - The Prussian loss: about 15,000 killed or wounded, 3,000 prisoners and 115 cannon (the
      journal); about 10,000 killed and wounded, 3,000 prisoners and 115 cannon (Bodart); 268
      officers (our arithmetic) and 57 guns without the battalion guns (Höpfner).
    - Also disputed: how far the reserve and the Guards were engaged, the fates of Schmettau and
      the Duke of Brunswick, and the hour Gudin's division crossed at Kösen.
- **Gaps and the targeted follow-ups.**
  - Saalfeld: side A's strength. The follow-up searched Foucart's OCR for 'SITUATION' and
    'Suchet', and read the documents of 11 October and the 2nd Bulletin. It found no return of
    the 5th corps. Foucher's guns, 2, 10 and 2, make Bodart's 14 (our arithmetic).
  - Jena: side A's loss. The follow-up searched Foucart's Jena documents (pp. 621–668) for
    'pertes' (12 hits) and 'tués' (24), read every hit, and selected and transcribed Suchet's
    report. The hits are mostly losses of single units. Two larger totals were read only in the
    OCR: the 4th corps's journal for the division Saint-Hilaire and the cavalry, and Ney's for
    his advanced guard. There is no return for the French army at Jena.
  - Auerstedt: how much of side B came into action. The follow-up read Höpfner's account
    (pp. 429–471), added the fight at Sulza to the selection, and transcribed four pages. In
    Höpfner the division Arnim fired no shot before the retreat was ordered, and the Guards and
    light troops at Sulza, 8 battalions (our arithmetic), fought a flank action. No source read
    counts the troops engaged.
- **Verification.** The three drafts were checked as a batch before release. The check:
  - read every claim of all three drafts against its cited passages and their context, and
    checked each page locator against the page images or the OCR's page heads;
  - checked every figure in a value against a page-image quote, and every English rendering of an
    OCR passage for digits and number words. The remaining flags are page numbers, number words,
    and figures read from page images.
  - fixed what it found:
    - Saalfeld:
      - renderings that dropped the sources' hedges ('il paraît', 'wohl', 'sans doute');
      - a footnote of Foucart's credited to Suchet's report;
      - a mistranslated 'sous le rapport de' and an overstated 'last attack';
      - the subject of Suchet's advance and the timing of the prince's march;
      - the family note, which had counted Mumpfling's report as a separate family.
    - Jena:
      - Rüchel's message to Tauentzien, whose addressee the value had left unnamed;
      - the cause of Rüchel's march: the prince's request, chosen over the King's summons;
      - the answer to Zeschwitz, which Höpfner does not attribute to the prince;
      - the prince's order to Holtzendorf, which its bearer could not deliver in time, and
        Holtzendorf's attack, now cited as made;
      - dropped hedges ('mußte', 'wohl', and Höpfner's judgment that waiting was wise);
      - 'alors' rendered as 'at last', and 'en venir aux mains' as 'the general engagement';
      - an overstated 'last favourable moment';
      - two renderings that ran beyond their quotes.

      17 citations were added, chiefly to show who spoke or acted.
    - Auerstedt:
      - the 'Laffetenkasten' rendering, Höpfner's Meile, and several referents;
      - four added citations;
      - an inspection note on p. 428 and an overstatement about chapter 10;
      - Foucart's page range (668–671).
  - found all 44 figure transcriptions cited. Of the selections' 17 sections, 16 are cited. The
    exception is Foucart's `second-bulletin`, whose text is cited from the transcriptions of its
    pp. 522 and 524.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,456 sources and 29 drafts (608 claims, 6 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 6.2 million tokens of the session's budget, roughly 2 million an entry. The
  batch held the group's three largest battles, two new families and 92 records. Its verification
  read every claim of all three drafts against its passages.
  - Of the group's other 20 entries, 14 are capitulations (one after an investment) and one is a
    siege. The other five are battles or combats: Halle, Lübeck, Czarnowo, Pultusk and Golymin.
  - The cohort's 296 remaining engagements would need about 600 million tokens at this batch's
    rate. At the finished groups' rates they would need about 170 to 200 million.
  - About 6.3 million remain in this session.

## 1806 first pass, batch 2b-i: Erfurt, Halle, Spandau and Prenzlau (2026-10-18)

The first part of batch 2b of `fourth-coalition 1806`, from the capitulation of Erfurt to Prince
Hohenlohe's at Prenzlau. Batch 2b is split in two so that each commit's verification stays
bounded; 2b-ii, from Stettin to Wolgast, follows.

| Entry | Engagement | Dates (Bodart) | Claims |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B373b | Erfurt (capitulation) | 15 October 1806 | 18 |
| B374a | Halle | 17 October 1806 | 19 |
| B374b | Spandau (capitulation) | 25 October 1806 | 18 |
| B374c | Prenzlau (capitulation) | 28 October 1806 | 18 |

The main session researched, drafted and verified the four without subagents. The work is
primary-verified; no separate review was made.

- **Sources.** Bodart and the two families of batch 2a, in their next volumes
  ([source additions](source-additions.md)):
  - Foucart's *Campagne de Prusse (1806): Prenzlow–Lubeck* (1890). For Erfurt it prints Murat's,
    Colonel Préval's, the major general's and the Emperor's letters and General Rott's report on
    the artillery of the reserves. For Halle it prints Bernadotte's and Dupont's letters of 18
    October, with extracts of the 1st corps's journal in Foucart's footnotes. For Spandau it prints
    Latour-Maubourg's note, Bertrand's two reports, Lannes's letter with the capitulation and the
    commandant's reply, and the Emperor's letter to Davout. For Prenzlau it prints Milhaud's note,
    Murat's letter to the Emperor and Grouchy's report.
  - Höpfner's *Der Krieg von 1806 und 1807*, Theil 1, Bd. 2 (2nd edition, 1855), the Prussian
    history. For Prenzlau it quotes Massenbach's memoir, follows Rittmeister v. d. Marwitz's report
    and cites Hohenlohe's.

  70 records were registered: two OCR parents, two selections and 33 page images (18 of Foucart,
  15 of Höpfner) with their figure transcriptions. The selections also hold the sections for
  2b-ii. Every figure in a value is read from a page image or from Bodart. Sums are labelled as
  ours.
  - Bodart matches these sources in places:
    - At Erfurt his 65 guns equal Höpfner's armament of the Petersberg.
    - At Halle his officers, guns and flags equal Höpfner's figures, and his killed and wounded and
      French loss come close to them (our arithmetic).
    - At Prenzlau his 10,000 men and 1,800 horses are Höpfner's estimate, and his 45 flags and 64
      guns are the French reports' figures as Höpfner gives them.

    The passages read do not show what he used.
- **Drafts.** 73 claims with 692 citations: 70 supported and 3 unknown. The unknowns are side A's
  losses at Erfurt, Spandau and Prenzlau, which no passage read gives; they are not set to zero.
  Every draft covers the nine dimensions.
  - Phases: 33 unresolved, 20 inherited, 10 commander-created and 10 post-outcome.
  - Posture: side A attacked in all four (A_attacked).
    - Erfurt and Spandau are tagged unresolved. The passages read show Murat's pursuit and summons
      at Erfurt and Lannes's investment and summonses at Spandau, but no order from above to attack
      either place.
    - Halle is commander-created: Bernadotte attacked by his own decision under a general order.
    - Prenzlau is commander-created: Murat ordered the attack on the suburb on the field.
  - Fortification: B_fortified for all four, tagged inherited.
    - Erfurt: the Petersberg and the Cyriaksburg had been put in a state of defence. A coding that
      counted only the fight on the heights before the town would give none_named.
    - Halle: the town's wall, gates and bridges. Counting the French defence of the captured gates
      would give both_fortified; counting only the fight for the position beyond the town would
      give none_named.
    - Spandau: the citadel in its lake, which surrendered before a shot was fired.
    - Prenzlau: the town's wall, palisades and gates, which checked the pursuit. Counting only the
      open position where the army capitulated would give none_named.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - Erfurt:
    - The men taken: 10,000 (Höpfner, Bodart); 6,000 to 7,000 surrendered, or at least 6,000
      prisoners and 5,000 wounded (Murat).
    - The guns: 65 (Bodart; Höpfner's armament of the Petersberg); 60 in battery and 20 to 25
      battalion guns (Murat).
    - The hour of Ney's infantry's arrival, which Höpfner and Murat give differently (OCR).
  - Halle:
    - Side B's strength: very nearly 11,000 in action (Höpfner); 13,700 (Bodart); nearly 25,000,
      with 20,000 defending the position (Bernadotte); 20,000 to 25,000 (the journal); about 30,000
      (Dupont).
    - The prisoners: about 5,000 (Bernadotte); 4,000 to 5,000 (Dupont); 4,200 (Bodart). Höpfner
      gives about 5000 killed, wounded, captured and missing, and cites the Duke of Württemberg's
      total of 3000 and some hundred.
    - The guns: at least 30 (Bernadotte); about thirty (Dupont); 11 without the regimental guns
      (Höpfner, Bodart); 8 (the Duke).
    - Side A's loss: under 800 by the French figure in Höpfner; about 800 (Bodart); 400 to 500 in
      Dupont's division (Dupont).
    - The divisions with the cavalry: Dupont and Drouet in Höpfner, Dupont and Rivaud in the French
      documents.
  - Spandau:
    - The garrison: not over 900 (Höpfner); 1,000 (Bodart); 1,200 (Lannes, and the Emperor's
      prisoners). Bertrand's informants give 800, or 1,400 with the 600 more apparently there (our
      arithmetic), and he saw no more than 400 on the ramparts.
    - The guns: 71 in Höpfner's ammunition count; 80 (the Emperor); 50, 60 or 90 (Bertrand's
      informants); a dozen in battery (Bertrand, OCR).
    - Whether the capitulation was concluded before the French occupied the walls (Höpfner says
      not).
  - Prenzlau:
    - Side B's strength and prisoners: Höpfner's table of 300 officers, 8831 men and 1616 horses,
      with some units not counted, and his estimate of not over 10,000 men and 1800 horses; 10,000
      with 400 officers (Bodart); 16,000 infantry and 6 cavalry regiments (Murat); 18,000 and 36
      guns (Milhaud's informants); 7-8000 (Massenbach).
    - Guns and flags: 64 and 45 (Bodart); 60 and 60 (Murat), with 18 guns taken by Grouchy; 8 guns
      in the suburb (Grouchy); 30 besides the regimental guns (Höpfner).
    - The terms: prisoners of war in Murat, released on parole in Höpfner; arms laid down in Murat,
      only stacked in Höpfner.
    - Whether Lannes's corps was near: the parlementaire's and Murat's claims, Taubenheim's
      voltigeurs, and Höpfner's note on Lannes's arrival at Templin.
- **Gaps.** No targeted follow-up was run in this sub-batch. Each draft names the record that
  could close its most consequential gap:
  - Erfurt: side B's strength and composition at the surrender. A Prussian or French return of the
    prisoners could close it. Side A's strength before the town is unknown.
  - Halle: side B's strength in action, and how its loss divides between the bridges and town, the
    position and the regiment Treskow's column. A page image of Höpfner's order of battle
    (pp. 44–45), whose table the OCR garbles, could give the units and their strengths.
  - Spandau: the garrison's size. Lannes's promised return of the place or a French list of the
    prisoners could settle it.
  - Prenzlau: side B's strength and loss at the capitulation. A French return of the prisoners or
    the sources of Höpfner's table could settle it.

  While drafting, Höpfner's Prenzlau selection was extended back to p. 177, so that the Prussian
  account of the fight in the suburb is citable.
- **Verification.** The four drafts were checked as a sub-batch before release. The check:
  - read every claim of Erfurt, Halle and Spandau against its cited passages and their context.
    Prenzlau had its full recheck while it was drafted, and its Foucart citations were rechecked
    for headings.
  - checked that the subject and attribution of each statement are in the claim's own quotes,
    including letter headings, signatures and the corps journal's closing attributions;
  - checked every figure in a value against a page-image or Bodart quote, and every rendering of an
    OCR passage for digits and number words. New this sub-batch, it also checked every figure in
    the open questions against the draft's page-image quotes. The remaining flags are page numbers,
    an edition number, number words, figures read from page images, labelled sums and one hour that
    the page image leaves uncertain.
  - fixed what it found:
    - Erfurt:
      - values and renderings that ran beyond their quotes: the Langensalza order, Höpfner's
        judgment and the Emperor's terms;
      - Foucart's `erfurt-16-oct` section, extended back to the major general's letter heading on
        p. 34;
      - the selection's note, which called Préval a general (he was a colonel), and the parent
        record's Erfurt pages (pp. 33–40);
      - figures read only in the OCR that the open questions restated.
    - Halle:
      - the description of the town, given as Foucart's note, is a footnote that closes with the
        corps journal's attribution. A paragraph on p. 100 that follows the journal extract has no
        attribution of its own and is now given as Foucart's footnote, not the journal's;
      - headings and closing attributions added, and subjects brought inside the quotes;
      - overstated verbs ('carried', 'stopped the pursuit', 'match', 'no food or drink');
      - the corps number in the journal's attribution, read only in the OCR, elided.
    - Spandau:
      - Latour-Maubourg's heading, missing from the transcription of p. 315, read from the image
        and added. The record was re-registered before it was committed.
      - Bertrand's two reports now cited with their headings and signatures;
      - the attribution of Lannes's postscript, and renderings that ran beyond their quotes.
    - The family notes now give the basis for Höpfner's reliance on the post-war judicial inquiries,
      the preface of his first volume, instead of asserting it for each place.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,526 sources and 33 drafts (681 claims, 9 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 2.4 million tokens, roughly 600,000 an entry. The sub-batch held one battle,
  three capitulations, two new volumes and 70 records.
  - The cohort's 292 remaining engagements would need about 175 million tokens at this rate.
  - About 3.9 million remain in this session.

## 1806 first pass, batch 2b-ii: Stettin, Pasewalk, Boldekow, Küstrin, Anklam and Wolgast (2026-10-18)

The second part of batch 2b of `fourth-coalition 1806`: the capitulations that followed Prince
Hohenlohe's at Prenzlau, from Stettin to Wolgast. Batch 2c, from Lübeck to Nienburg, follows.

| Entry | Engagement | Dates (Bodart) | Claims |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B374d | Stettin (capitulation) | 29 October 1806 | 18 |
| B375a | Pasewalk (capitulation) | 29 October 1806 | 18 |
| B375b | Boldekow (capitulation) | 30 October 1806 | 18 |
| B375c | Küstrin (capitulation) | 1 November 1806 | 18 |
| B375d | Anklam (capitulation) | 1 November 1806 | 18 |
| B375e | Wolgast (capitulation) | 3 November 1806 | 18 |

The main session researched, drafted and verified the six without subagents. The work is
primary-verified; no separate review was made.

- **Sources.** Bodart and the two families of sub-batch 2b-i, whose selections already held these
  sections ([source additions](source-additions.md)):
  - Foucart's *Campagne de Prusse (1806): Prenzlow–Lubeck* (1890), the French side's documents:
    - Stettin: Murat's letter with the capitulation, Belliard's and Lasalle's letters and the 24th
      Bulletin. Murat's further orders and Lannes's and Belliard's continuations were read only in
      the OCR.
    - Pasewalk: Murat's letters of 28 and 30 October, Milhaud's letter to Belliard with Foucart's
      note, and the 24th Bulletin.
    - Boldekow: Lannes's and Murat's letters of 31 October. Lannes's further letter was read only
      in the OCR.
    - Küstrin: the Emperor's letter to the Grand Duke of Berg and the 25th Bulletin, both of 2
      November.
    - Anklam: Lasalle's, Belliard's, Beker's and Murat's letters and the 25th Bulletin, with further
      letters read only in the OCR. Murat's letters pass on Beker's reports.
    - Wolgast: Murat's letter to the Emperor, Belliard's orders and report, Boyer's and Déry's
      letters, the capitulation, and Colonel Dommanget's report on Guérin's detachment.
  - Höpfner's *Der Krieg von 1806 und 1807*, Theil 1, Bd. 2 (2nd edition, 1855), the Prussian
    history: its account of the capitulations after Prenzlau (pp. 209–236) and of Küstrin
    (pp. 324–328). For Anklam it quotes Mathieu Dumas's account of the French side. The Stettin
    passage names no source beyond "a reliable report" on the state of the works, and the other
    passages name none.

  94 records were registered: 47 page images (24 of Foucart, 23 of Höpfner) with their figure
  transcriptions. No parent or selection was added. Every figure in a value is read from a page
  image or from Bodart. Sums are labelled as ours.
  - Bodart matches Höpfner's figures at every place (our arithmetic):
    - Stettin: his 5,300 with 100 officers is Höpfner's 100 officers and 5184 men rounded up, and
      his 281 guns are Höpfner's 187 serviceable and 94 for emergencies.
    - Pasewalk: his 185 officers, 8 guns and 1 wagon are Höpfner's, and his 2,100 horses round up
      Höpfner's 2087. His 4,200 is close to Höpfner's 4228 officers and men.
    - Boldekow: his 25 guns, 48 wagons and 800 horses are Höpfner's, and his about 600 men round
      up Höpfner's 564.
    - Küstrin: his 2,400 men and 92 guns equal Höpfner's about 2400 and his 80 and 12 guns.
    - Anklam: his 1,100 infantry and 1,100 cavalry are Höpfner's 1100 infantry and 1073 horses
      rounded up, and his 2,200 is their sum.
    - Wolgast: his 500 wagons are Höpfner's, his 2,500 men equal the lower of Höpfner's figures
      for the column's horses (2500–3000), and his Colonel Daru is Höpfner's Colonel Darü.

    The passages read do not show what he used.
- **Drafts.** 108 claims with 1,017 citations: 104 supported and 4 unknown. The unknowns are side
  A's losses at Stettin, Pasewalk, Boldekow and Küstrin, which no passage read gives; they are not
  set to zero. Every draft covers the nine dimensions.
  - Phases: 45 unresolved, 37 inherited, 12 commander-created and 14 post-outcome.
  - Posture: side A attacked in all six (A_attacked), and side B capitulated.
    - Stettin and Boldekow are tagged unresolved. At Stettin, Murat sent Belliard to summon the
      commandant, but Lasalle had begun the summons before Belliard came; the passages read do not
      settle whether it was Lasalle's own initiative or Murat's order. At Boldekow they do not show
      who set the French advance, and the contact was made by the Prussian lieutenant sent out to
      reconnoitre.
    - Pasewalk, Küstrin, Anklam and Wolgast are tagged inherited:
      - Pasewalk: Milhaud's approach was set by the pursuit before it, and the parleys were begun
        by the Prussian officers.
      - Küstrin: Davout ordered Gudin's division against the place.
      - Anklam: Murat ordered the move and the summons.
      - Wolgast: Murat ordered the pursuit over the Peene, as the order to Lasalle to which
        Foucart's note on p. 627 refers shows for the hussars.
  - Fortification:
    - Stettin and Küstrin: B_fortified, tagged inherited, for their permanent works.
    - Wolgast: B_fortified, tagged inherited, on the capitulation's words "the place" and "the
      glacis" alone. Every other passage read would give none_named.
    - Pasewalk, Boldekow and Anklam: none_named, tagged unresolved. At Anklam the passages name a
      bridge that could be drawn up and a closed town, but no works.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - Stettin:
    - The garrison: 100 officers and 5184 men (Höpfner); 5,300 with 100 officers (Bodart); 5,500
      taken (Lasalle); 6,000 (the Bulletin).
    - The guns: 187 serviceable and 94 for emergencies (Höpfner); 281 (Bodart); 120 (Lasalle); 160
      (the Bulletin).
    - The hour of the capitulation. It is dated 6 in the evening of the 29th and was approved by
      Belliard that night; Belliard reported it at 1 in the morning; Höpfner has it carried out in
      the night to the 30th, with Lasalle in the town at 6 in the morning before it was signed.
      Murat's report is dated 2 in the morning in its dateline and 5 in the morning in Höpfner.
    - Romberg's rank: Major General (Bodart); General-Lieutenant (Höpfner); lieutenant-général (the
      capitulation).
    - Bodart's "without a stroke of the sword" against Belliard's few cannon shots.
  - Pasewalk:
    - Side B's strength: 185 officers and 4043 men with 2087 horses, 8 guns and 1 powder wagon,
      and Hagen's 2000 infantry (Höpfner); 4,200 with 185 officers, 2,100 horses, 8 guns and 1
      ammunition wagon (Bodart); 2,000 cavalry, 3,000 infantry and 5,000 prisoners (Milhaud); 6,000
      (Murat and the Bulletin).
    - Side A: 600 horse (Milhaud); 700 (Bodart).
    - The flags: 36 (Milhaud); none counted by Höpfner or Bodart.
    - Who received the capitulation: Milhaud (Bodart and the French letters), or a negotiator with
      powers from the Grand Duke, the draft going to him at Löcknitz for ratification (Höpfner).
  - Boldekow:
    - The men: 564 from Höpfner's figures (our arithmetic); about 600 (Bodart); 1,500 and 200
      dragoons (Lannes). Murat counts 60 mounted gunners taken separately.
    - The guns: 25 (Höpfner, Bodart); 30 (Lannes). The wagons: 48 ammunition wagons (Höpfner,
      Bodart); 60 caissons and many wagons (Lannes).
    - Whether the escort capitulated (Bodart) or had gone off across the Peene (Höpfner).
    - The park's destination (Spandau, Lannes; Stettin, Murat) and its horses (starved, Höpfner;
      well horsed, Lannes).
  - Küstrin:
    - The garrison: about 2400 with 1600 fit (Höpfner); 2,400 (Bodart); 4,000 prisoners (the
      Emperor, the Bulletin).
    - The guns: 80 on the walls and 12 in reserve (Höpfner), 92 by our arithmetic; 92 (Bodart); 80
      (the Emperor); 90 (the Bulletin).
    - Side A: 1,500 under Gauthier (Bodart); the 85th regiment, left under Gauthier when Gudin's
      division was recalled (Höpfner). Who summoned the place: Gudin (Höpfner) or Davout (the
      Bulletin).
    - Bodart's "without striking a blow" against Höpfner's fight at the bridgehead on 31 October,
      in which Lieutenant Falkenhayn was shot dead.
  - Anklam:
    - Side B's strength: 1100 infantry and 1073 horses (Höpfner); 2,200 (Bodart); 1,200 and some
      mounted cavalry and more than 1,500 infantry (Beker's count); 4,000 (the Bulletin and Dumas).
      Earlier estimates: the postillon's 6,000; Beker's 2,000 and then 3,000 with 1,000 to 1,200
      cavalry; Murat's 3,000 to 4,000 with 1,400 or 1,500 horses. Murat's 400 taken in the tail and
      Beker's more than 300 cavalry and as many infantry may or may not be inside these.
    - Side B's guns: none (the postillon); guns (Murat); 4 horsed guns (Beker's count); none named
      by Höpfner.
    - The course of the action:
      - Höpfner: a bridge held until a parlementaire came, and Hiller's detachments captured outside
        the town, with no charge.
      - Murat on 31 October: only the tail taken, the rest across the Peene behind a raised
        drawbridge. On 1 November: a very vigorous charge in the town.
      - The Bulletin and Dumas: a charge on the plain and an entry into the town with the enemy,
        which Höpfner calls "so many words, so many untruths".
    - The French loss: an adjudant sous-officier and a few dragoons (Murat), in a charge that
      Höpfner's narrative does not have.
    - Side B's commander: General v. Bila (Bodart); the brothers Bila I and Bila II (Höpfner); 2
      generals (Beker).
    - Names as printed: Becker and Beker; Boussard, Boussart and Broussart.
  - Wolgast:
    - The strength. Bodart: 2,500, mostly train troops, with 500 wagons. Höpfner: 500 wagons and
      2500–3000 horses for Langwerth's column at Friedland. Déry: about 2,000 men, 84 officers and
      4,000 to 5,000 horses. The French report: 800 cavalry depot men, 1,500 infantry and 300
      vehicles. Murat: an escort of about 1,000 men for the equipages that passed Demmin. None says
      how many came under Langwerth's capitulation and how many under Prittwitz's.
    - The war chest: taken (Bodart); embarked at Anklam on the 29th (Höpfner); the King's treasure
      of 10 millions gone 6 days before, and a single regimental chest of 1,000 francs found (Déry).
    - The French commander: Colonel Daru (Bodart); Colonel Darü's raiding party (Höpfner); Déry,
      chef d'escadron in his letter's heading and the Grand Duke's aide-de-camp in the
      capitulation, which he signed.
    - One capitulation (Bodart) or two, Langwerth's at Anklam and Prittwitz's at Wolgast (Höpfner).
    - The date: 3 November (Bodart); 2 and 3 November (Höpfner); signed on 2 November to be
      carried out the next day (the capitulation).
    - The town's country: Prussia in Bodart's locator; Swedish Pomerania in the French report, in
      Murat and in Höpfner's Swedish officers.
- **Gaps.** Two targeted follow-ups were run:
  - Küstrin: an OCR search of Foucart's volume for Küstrin, Gauthier, Ingersleben and Duplin found
    no French report of the capitulation besides the Emperor's letter and the 25th Bulletin.
    Davout's own correspondence, a further family, was not read.
  - Wolgast: the order to which Foucart's note on p. 627 refers, and the French letters around it
    (pp. 618–621), were read. They settled the posture's phase as inherited and gave the dragoon
    detachment as 50 men from each of its two regiments. The hussars' strength is still unknown.

  Each draft names the record that could close its most consequential gap:
  - Stettin: side A's strength. The only figure is the 800 cavalry and 2 guns before which the
    garrison laid down its arms. A return of Lasalle's brigade or Murat's situation reports could
    settle it.
  - Pasewalk: side A's strength, 600 (Milhaud) or 700 (Bodart). A return of Milhaud's brigade could
    settle it.
  - Boldekow: who and how many side B were; side A's force is not given at all. A French return of
    the prisoners, or Lannes's outpost reports, could settle both.
  - Küstrin: side A's strength, and with it whether a garrison of about 2400 surrendered to a
    single regiment. A return of the 85th for 1 November could settle it.
  - Anklam: side B's strength, and what was taken in the fighting as against at the capitulation.
    Beker's promised return, or the return Belliard asked for, could settle it.
  - Wolgast: the size of each side. A French return of the prisoners could settle side B.
- **Verification.** The six drafts were checked as a sub-batch before release. The check:
  - read every claim of Stettin, Pasewalk, Boldekow, Küstrin and Anklam against its cited passages
    and their context. Wolgast had its full recheck while it was drafted.
  - checked that the subject and attribution of each statement are in the claim's own quotes.
    Where Höpfner refers to the subject only by a pronoun, the claim also cites the sentence that
    names it.
  - checked every figure in a value against a page-image or Bodart quote, every rendering of an
    OCR passage for digits and number words, and every figure in the open questions against the
    draft's page-image quotes.
  - read on the page images figures that the OCR misreads or loses:
    - in Höpfner: "am 26." (OCR "20.") on p. 225, "1. November" on p. 226, "1000 Pferden" on
      p. 230, "1¼ M." (OCR "14 M.") on p. 235, "könne" (OCR "konne") on p. 327, and the table on
      p. 214, which the OCR garbles;
    - in Foucart: "500" (OCR "Ô00") on p. 530, datelines whose "30" and "1806" the OCR gives as
      "80" and "1808", and Déry's "2 novembre" (OCR "8").
  - fixed what it found:
    - Stettin: the words "seen and approved the present capitulation to be carried out", printed
      above Romberg's and Lasalle's names, are no longer read as Lasalle's approval; whose approval
      they record is not shown. Also "the Emperor's hussars", a missing citation, and an open
      question's note on a heading read only in the OCR.
    - Pasewalk: wording brought into line with its quotes, among it "found there", "came back"
      and "not practicable", and subjects brought inside the quotes.
    - Boldekow: Braun II's name brought inside the quotes; "Friedland, in Mecklenburg", which no
      passage read gives, removed; "by the time of the capitulation".
    - Küstrin: renderings that ran beyond their quotes; the ground restated from the passages
      (the abatis and wolf pits, and the bridge the commandant had already set on fire); Bodart's
      "without striking a blow" now cited in the posture claim; "the Grand Duke of Berg" for Murat,
      whom no quote there names.
    - Anklam: anchor sentences cited where Höpfner names the subject only by a pronoun; renderings
      of passages the claim does not cite removed, and Beker's report rendered in parts matched to
      the claims that cite them; Beker's charge "in person"; the French loss put "in a charge that
      Höpfner's narrative does not have", not one he denies.
    - Wolgast, while it was drafted: names, units and attributions corrected against their quotes,
      Langwerth marked as named by context where the OCR line that names him is garbled, and a note
      that Bodart's locator does not say whether it describes 1806.
    - Across the sub-batch: the attribution of the remark printed after Déry's letter on p. 627;
      the cross-note on Belliard's wagons of flour, grain and baggage, which no draft assigns to
      Anklam or Wolgast; Boldekow's 564, labelled as our arithmetic.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,620 sources and 39 drafts (789 claims, 13 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 3.5 million tokens, roughly 590,000 an entry. The sub-batch held six
  capitulations and 94 records, with no new volume.
  - The cohort's 286 remaining engagements would need about 170 million tokens at this rate.
  - About 0.35 million remain in this session.

## 1806 first pass, sub-batch 2c-i: Ratkau (2026-10-18)

The first part of batch 2c of `fourth-coalition 1806`: Blücher's capitulation at Ratkau on the
morning after the fight for Lübeck. It was taken alone because the session's remaining budget
could hold one bounded capitulation but not the Lübeck battle. Lübeck (B376a), Magdeburg (B376c),
Hameln (B376d) and Nienburg (B377a) follow in 2c.

| Entry | Engagement | Dates (Bodart) | Claims |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B376b | Ratkau (capitulation) | 7 November 1806 | 18 |

The main session researched, drafted and verified the entry without subagents. The work is
primary-verified; no separate review was made.

- **Sources.** Bodart and the two families of batch 2b, whose cited passages were all read from
  registered page images ([source additions](source-additions.md)):
  - Foucart t. II, the French side's documents:
    - the Grand Duke of Berg's letter to the Emperor and Belliard's letter to the major general,
      both of 7 November (p. 757);
    - the rest of Belliard's letter and the capitulation (p. 759);
    - Bernadotte's letter to the Emperor (p. 783) and Soult's letter (p. 785), both of 8 November;
    - the 29th Bulletin of 9 November (pp. 799 and 802).
  - Höpfner Bd. 2, pp. 297 and 300–305, the Prussian account of the night at Ratkau, the surprise
    at Schwartau, the summons, the negotiation and the capitulation. For the prisoners it cites
    C. v. W.'s *Operationsplan der preußisch-sächsischen Armee im Jahre 1806*, which was not read.

  26 records were registered: 13 page images (6 of Foucart, 7 of Höpfner) with their figure
  transcriptions. No parent or selection was added. No figure in a value is taken from the OCR.
- **Drafts.** 18 claims with 157 citations: 17 supported and 1 unknown, side A's losses, which no
  passage read gives and which is not set to zero. Phases: 7 unresolved (with the unknown),
  5 commander_created, 4 inherited and 2 post_outcome. Posture is coded `A_attacked` as
  commander_created, from the French moves of the morning of the 7th: the Grand Duke of Berg's
  encirclement and Bernadotte's preparation to attack. Fortification is `none_named` and
  unresolved. The boundary note keeps the fight for Lübeck (B376a) out of the
  entry, leaves the prisoners of the Schwartau night surprise for B376a to place, does not add the
  Bulletin's 16,000 for both days, and excludes Travemünde's surrender of 8 November and Pelet's
  capitulation of 11–12 November.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - Side B's strength: Höpfner's about 8000 men left, and C. v. W.'s 4050 infantry and 3760
    cavalry, 7810 in our arithmetic; Bodart's 9,000 with about 4,000 cavalry; Belliard's estimate
    of 12 or 15,000 prisoners; the Bulletin's 16,000 for the two days together.
  - The squadrons that laid down their arms: 52 for Belliard and Bernadotte, 53 for Soult.
  - Side A: Bodart's about 40,000, or the 80,000 the French generals named to Blücher.
  - Who summoned Blücher, and on whose authority the capitulation was accepted.
  - The name: Ratkau in Bodart and Höpfner, Ratzkaw in the capitulation, Schwartau in the Bulletin.
- **Gaps.** No targeted follow-up was run. The most consequential gap is side A's strength, which
  only Bodart's figure and the generals' claim give; the fold-out situation tables after Höpfner's
  p. 939 are the lead and were not read. Deferred: Belliard's promised return, the original
  capitulation, the rest of Bernadotte's and Soult's letters (pp. 784 and 786), Compans's order on
  p. 763 (read only in the OCR), the C. v. W. work, Höpfner's p. 306 and the inquiry records.
- **Verification.** The draft was checked before release. The check:
  - reread every cited passage against its page image and transcription, and read the figures
    3760, 8000, 27 and 52, and 53 and 6,000 from full-resolution crops; the OCR's 3700 for the
    cavalry is corrected to the printed 3760;
  - fixed what it found: anchor sentences that name Blücher cited where Höpfner says only "the
    General"; "the Grand Duke of Berg", since no quote names Murat; the Grand Duke's remark that
    reasons are not customary in a capitulation, now cited where the claim uses it; the date of
    Bernadotte's letter; renderings that ran beyond their quotes, among them the men and horses
    "exhausted and shaken"; the flags, whose source letter also covers the 6th; and the Bulletin's
    figures, now given for the two days and for the day at Lübeck as it gives them.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,646 sources and 40 drafts (807 claims, 14 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 0.3 million tokens for the one entry and its 26 records, with no new volume.
  - A single capitulation is not a fair rate for the battles to come, and the earlier estimate of
    165 to 190 million tokens for the cohort's other 285 engagements stands.
  - Under 0.03 million remain in this session, so Lübeck to Nienburg are left to the next one.

The second part of batch 2c of `fourth-coalition 1806`: the storm of Lübeck on 6 November, the
day before the capitulation at Ratkau (B376b). Magdeburg (B376c), Hameln (B376d) and Nienburg
(B377a) complete batch 2c.

| Entry | Engagement | Dates (Bodart) | Claims |

The main session researched, drafted and verified the entry without subagents. The work is
primary-verified; no separate review was made.

- **Sources.** Bodart and the two families of batch 2b, whose cited passages were all read from
  registered page images ([source additions](source-additions.md)):
  - Foucart t. II, the French side's documents:
    - Bernadotte's letter to the Grand Duke of Berg from Schönberg, 4 in the morning of
      6 November (p. 732);
    - his letter to the major general of 7 November (pp. 732–735 and 738–740), with the 1st corps
      journal in the footnote of p. 735;
    - Soult's letter to the Emperor of 6 November (pp. 741–743), with the 4th corps's report and
      General Lariboisière in its footnotes;
    - the Grand Duke of Berg's letter to the Emperor of 6 November, 9 in the evening
      (pp. 744–745);
    - the 29th Bulletin of 9 November (p. 801, with pp. 799 and 802 from sub-batch 2c-i).
  - Höpfner Bd. 2, pp. 272–273, 277, 279, 282–284, 287–288, 292–295 and 299: the Prussian account
    from Gadebusch to the fall of the town, Major Ende's capitulation and Major Witzleben's
    detachment. The surprise of Schwartau is cited from pp. 300–301, registered for 2c-i.

  54 records were registered: 27 page images (13 of Foucart, 14 of Höpfner) with their figure
  transcriptions. No parent or selection was added. No figure in a value is taken from the OCR.
- **Drafts.** 19 claims with 342 citations, all supported. Phases: 7 unresolved,
  5 commander_created, 4 post_outcome and 3 inherited.
  - Posture is coded `A_attacked` as commander_created. The attack followed the marshals' decision
    of 4 November to leave Blücher no time and Bernadotte's own order on seeing the defences.
  - Fortification is coded `B_fortified` as inherited: side B held the town's permanent walls,
    ditches and gates.
  - The boundary note places the night surprise of Schwartau in this entry, as B376b left it to
    do. Its prisoners, most of the regiment Kunheim by Höpfner, are reported here and not added to
    Bodart's figures. The note also:
    - keeps the Swedish detachment taken at Schlutup apart from the Prussians where the sources
      do;
    - does not split the Bulletin's 16,000 prisoners for both days;
    - excludes Ratkau (B376b), Travemünde's surrender and Pelet's capitulation.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - Side B's strength:
    - Bodart: 15,000.
    - The 1st corps journal: about 20,000 on the rampart and around the town, with about 5,000
      outside.
    - Bernadotte, writing at 4 that morning: very few men at Lübeck.
    - Höpfner: 13 battalions, later reinforcements and 52 guns.
  - The prisoners of the day:
    - about 4,000 (Bodart and the Bulletin);
    - more than 5,000 including the Swedes (Bernadotte);
    - at least 6,000 (Soult);
    - 5,000 (the Grand Duke of Berg);
    - 5—6000 lodged in the churches (Höpfner).
  - The guns: from Höpfner's 22 without the regimental cannon to the Bulletin's 60.
  - The Swedes taken: more than 600 (Bernadotte), 1,000 or 1,200 (the Grand Duke), 1,600 (the
    Bulletin).
  - Side A's loss: Bodart's about 2,000, and Bernadotte's more than 1,500 killed or wounded in the
    1st corps.
  - The squadrons cut off after the town fell.
  - Who directed side A's attack: the Grand Duke of Berg principally, in Soult's letter, or the
    marshals' dispositions, in the Grand Duke's.
- **Gaps.** No targeted follow-up was run.
  - The most consequential gap is side A's strength. Bodart's 30,000 is the only count read, and
    the French letters name divisions without numbers. The fold-out situation tables in Foucart's
    annexes, after p. 939, are the lead for this entry and for Ratkau; they were not read.
  - Deferred:
    - Foucart pp. 736–737, the rest of Bernadotte's account of the storm with the 1st corps
      journal, read only in the OCR;
    - the untranscribed parts of pp. 733 and 739–741, and the Grand Duke's postscript (p. 746);
    - Höpfner's pages between those transcribed, among them the fight at the Burgthor, and his
      plan of Lübeck.
- **Verification.** The pages and the draft were checked before release. The check:
  - transcribed every figure from the page images, mostly on full-resolution crops, and corrected
    the OCR's misreadings to the print:
    - 25 pieces on the Burgthor front, not 26;
    - Legrand's 2,500 prisoners, not 600;
    - Höpfner's 4—5000, not 45000;
    - his ½4 Uhr, not 4 Uhr;
  - fixed the date note of p. 733 before registration: it had named the wrong page for the date
    line of Bernadotte's letter;
  - reread every cited passage against its transcription;
  - added three citations that were missing:
    - the ships carrying Swedish troops at Schlutup, which the strength claim names;
    - the 4th corps's attribution of the Fackenburg footnote;
    - the Hamburg-road column's surrender to Soult's light cavalry, now worded as the Grand Duke
      gives it.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,700 sources and 41 drafts (826 claims, 14 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 1.0 million tokens for the one entry and its 54 records, by the session's token

## 1806 first pass, sub-batch 2c-iii: Magdeburg (2026-10-18)

The third part of batch 2c of `fourth-coalition 1806`: the investment and capitulation of
Magdeburg, from Ney's first summons on 23 October to the garrison's defile on 11 November. Hameln
(B376d) and Nienburg (B377a) complete batch 2c.

| Entry | Engagement | Dates (Bodart) | Claims |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B376c | Magdeburg (capitulation) | 8 November 1806 | 18 |

The main session researched, drafted and verified the entry without subagents. The work is
primary-verified; no separate review was made.

- **Sources.** Bodart and the two families of batch 2b, whose cited passages were all read from
  registered page images ([source additions](source-additions.md)):
  - Foucart t. II, the French side's documents:
    - Ney's letters to the major general of 23 and 25 October and of 2, 3 and 7 November
      (pp. 276, 347–348, 642–643, 677–679 and 764–765), and his daily report on the blockade of
      2 November (pp. 644–645);
    - General Marchand's letter to Ney of 2 November (pp. 643–644);
    - the Emperor's letters to Ney of 7 and 12 November (pp. 766–767 and 827) and to Lannes of
      9 November (p. 795);
    - Ney's two letters to the Emperor of 8 November, with the armistice of 7 November and the
      capitulation's articles 1 to 4 in their footnotes (pp. 787–791), and his letter of
      11 November, with General Roguet's memoirs in a footnote (pp. 824–826);
    - the 29th, 30th and 31st Bulletins (p. 803, with p. 799 from sub-batch 2c-i; pp. 806
      and 827).
  - Höpfner Bd. 2, pp. 330–336: the Prussian account of the fortress, the governor, the
    investment, the meeting of 6 November, the armistice, the capitulation and the booty. In this
    section he cites Ney's correspondence captured at Gutstadt in 1807 and the records of the
    inquiry into the capitulation.

  66 records were registered: 33 page images (26 of Foucart, 7 of Höpfner) with their figure
  transcriptions. No parent or selection was added. No figure in a value is taken from the OCR.
- **Drafts.** 18 claims with 438 citations, all supported. Phases: 8 unresolved, 5 inherited,
  3 post_outcome and 2 commander_created.
  - Posture is coded `A_attacked` and left unresolved. Ney invested, bombarded and summoned the
    fortress, and the garrison's sorties were counterattacks within the siege. The passages read
    show no order from above to invest or attack. The Emperor's order to bombard is dated after
    Ney's trial bombardment and refers to an earlier letter that was not found.
  - Fortification is coded `both_fortified` and left unresolved: the permanent fortress against
    Ney's entrenched posts under its fire. Counting only the defended fortress would give
    `B_fortified`.
  - Side A's losses are a partial post_outcome claim. Ney's daily report gives 2 killed and
    3 wounded on 30 and 31 October and none for the other days it covers; no French loss is read
    from 3 November on.
  - The boundary note counts the remnants Prince Hohenlohe left in the garrison here, not at
    Prenzlau (B374c), and excludes Hameln and Nienburg.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - The garrison:
    - Höpfner's return: 24,118; Bodart: 24,000;
    - Ney: at least 8,000 (23 October), 15,000 (2 November), 20,000 with the sick and wounded
      (8 November) and 22,000 at the defile;
    - Williben: more than 16,000, which Ney thought exaggerated;
    - the Emperor and the 29th and 30th Bulletins: 16,000;
    - Roguet: 20,000 prisoners reached the Rhine.
  - The guns: 577 (Höpfner's return and Bodart), 700 (Ney on 11 November, and Höpfner from Ney's
    report), or 800 and about 800 (Ney on 8 November, the Emperor, the 30th and 31st Bulletins).
  - The generals: Bodart's 16 released on parole, the number Höpfner names (our count), against
    20 in Ney, the 31st Bulletin and Höpfner's figures from Ney's report.
  - The date and hour:
    - the 8th in Bodart, Höpfner, Ney, the capitulation's date line and the 30th Bulletin; the
      7th in the Emperor's letter of 9 November;
    - 1 o'clock in Höpfner, the evening in Ney;
    - the Ulrich gate on the 10th (article 2 and Höpfner), the gates on the 9th (the 30th
      Bulletin).
  - The armistice: signed for side B by du Troussel and Le Blanc in Foucart's text; Höpfner adds
    Renouard.
  - The night of 4 to 5 November: French attacks on the Neustadt (Höpfner) or a Prussian sortie
    repulsed (Ney).
  - The inhabitants: no hindrance to the defence (Höpfner), against the burghers' threat of
    revolt and a party for capitulation (Ney and the 29th Bulletin).
  - The regimental chests: in the fortress (the Emperor and the 29th Bulletin) or gone with
    Hohenlohe's corps (Ney, 11 November).
  - The defile: 4 columns from 9 to 3 (Ney), 4 columns with 20 cavalrymen each (Höpfner), or
    8 columns finished at 6 in the evening (Roguet).
- **Gaps.** No targeted follow-up was run.
  - The most consequential gap is side A's strength. Bodart's 16,000 is the only total read.
    Höpfner says Ney's corps hardly had the garrison's strength and had 900 cavalry; Ney's letters
    give his cavalry and detachments but no infantry count. The fold-out situation tables in
    Foucart's annexes, after p. 939, remain the lead.
  - Deferred:
    - the capitulation's articles 5 to 18 (pp. 790–791), the end of the Emperor's letter to
      Lannes (p. 795) and the rest of Roguet's footnote (p. 825);
    - seen only in the OCR: the Emperor's letter to Ney of 9 November (p. 794), his letter of
      10 November on receiving the capitulation (p. 811), the major general's orders after the
      defile (p. 820) and the administration's use of the Magdeburg magazines (pp. 863–866);
    - not found: Ney's plan of the blockade, and the earlier letter in which the Emperor told Ney
      that the bombardment should keep increasing.
- **Verification.** The pages and the draft were checked before release. The check:
  - transcribed every figure from the page images, mostly on full-resolution crops, and corrected
    the OCR's misreadings to the print, among them:
    - the date lines of Ney's letters of 23 and 25 October and 2 and 3 November (pp. 276, 347,
      642 and 677);
    - the year 1806 in the armistice's closing line, read as 1836 (p. 788);
    - Höpfner's 3 infantry regiments, read as 23 (p. 336);
  - left the damaged second figure of the hour in the capitulation's article 3 unread
    ('1[?]') and out of the values;
  - searched the volume for further French documents and added the 29th and 30th Bulletins'
    Magdeburg passages;
  - reread every cited passage against its transcription and fixed what the reread found:
    - the Emperor's sentence on the Russians now cites the sentence that names them;
    - his sentence on the letters from Stettin does not name the place; the draft now says so;
    - Ney's letter of 25 October gives Colbert's crossing as a plan for the next day;
    - Höpfner says a formal council of war was expected; the draft no longer says none was held;
    - the demolitions of 29 October and the foraging of the 31st are now the defenders' and the
      garrison's, as Höpfner gives them, not Kleist's;
    - Ney's report would burn Sudenburg and the Closterberg, not the Neustadt;
  - added passages the reread showed were needed:
    - the armistice's parties and their agreement on the capitulation's main points;
    - Marchand's judgment of Kleist;
    - Ney's request for more light cavalry and the detachments coming from Erfurt;
    - Ney's information of 11 November on the regimental chests and the Hesse treasure;
    - the Emperor's leave for a Prussian officer to confirm the fall of Küstrin and Stettin;
    - Höpfner's six days of confusion in the town.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,766 sources and 42 drafts (844 claims, 14 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 1.5 million tokens for the one entry and its 66 records, by the session's
  token counter.
  - That is above Lübeck's 1.0 million. Magdeburg has 33 pages of documents and a seven-page
    Prussian account.
  - The estimate for the cohort's other 283 engagements stays at about 165 to 190 million
    tokens.
- **Corrections to the running notes.** The notes placed Höpfner's Magdeburg section at

## 1806 first pass, sub-batch 2c-iv: Hameln and Nienburg (2026-10-18)

The last part of batch 2c of `fourth-coalition 1806`: the blockade and capitulation of Hameln and
the capitulation of Nienburg, the two Prussian fortresses on the Weser. Batch 2c is complete.

| Entry | Engagement | Dates (Bodart) | Claims |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B376d | Hameln (capitulation) | 20 November 1806 | 18 |
| B377a | Nienburg (capitulation) | 26 November 1806 | 18, 1 unknown |

The main session researched, drafted and verified both entries without subagents. The work is
primary-verified; no separate review was made.

- **Sources.** Bodart and the two families of batch 2b. Foucart's *Campagne de Pologne*, t. I, the
  same author's next volume, joins his family. Every cited passage was read from a registered page
  image ([source additions](source-additions.md)):
  - Foucart t. II, the French side's documents:
    - the Emperor's letters to the King of Holland of 22 and 31 October (pp. 222–223 and 559), to
      Mortier of 7 November (pp. 771–772) and to Ney of 9 November (p. 794);
    - Mortier's letters to the Emperor of 12 and 13 November (pp. 834–835 and 845–846);
    - the 29th and 32nd Bulletins (p. 805, with p. 799 from sub-batch 2c-i; pp. 848–849).
  - *Campagne de Pologne*, t. I (1882):
    - in a footnote on pp. 132–133, the 33rd Bulletin with the suspension of arms of 16 November,
      whose article 4 names Hameln and Nienburg, and an extract of the 35th Bulletin;
    - the Emperor's letter to the Grand Duke of Berg of 3 December, which reports the surrender of
      Glogau and Nienburg (pp. 215–216).
  - Höpfner Bd. 2, chapter 19, on the Weser: Hameln, pp. 347–378, and Nienburg, pp. 378–383. For
    Hameln he cites General Savary's report for the prisoners and an enemy officer's statement for
    the French loss of 7 November. He gives Napoleon's orders of 13 and 18 November and the French
    commanders' letters in summary, and says that as good as nothing about the enemy's movements
    in western Germany has come from the French side. For Nienburg he names no source.

  91 records were registered: the *Campagne de Pologne* OCR parent and 45 page images (13 of
  Foucart t. II, 4 of *Campagne de Pologne* and 28 of Höpfner) with their figure transcriptions.
  No figure in a value is taken from the OCR.
- **Drafts.** 36 claims with 512 citations: 35 supported and 1 unknown.
  - Hameln: 18 claims with 353 citations. Phases: 8 unresolved, 5 inherited, 3 post_outcome and
    2 commander_created.
  - Nienburg: 18 claims with 159 citations. Phases of the 17 supported claims: 8 unresolved,
    5 inherited, 2 commander_created and 2 post_outcome.
  - Both postures are coded `A_attacked` and left unresolved. At Hameln side B's small actions
    fell within the blockade; at Nienburg no passage read describes any fighting.
  - Both fortifications are coded `B_fortified` and tagged inherited. Napoleon's orders, as
    Höpfner gives them, required the troops before Hameln to entrench, but no passage read shows
    their works; counting them would give `both_fortified`.
  - Side A's losses: at Hameln a partial post_outcome claim, an enemy officer's 'some 50' for the
    action of 7 November as Höpfner gives it; at Nienburg unknown, not zero.
  - Each boundary note keeps its capitulation to its own fortress. The suspension of arms of
    16 November, never ratified, is context for both.
- **Disputes kept visible.**
  - Hameln's garrison:
    - Höpfner: over 5000 in the garrison and 4000 in Lecoq's corps, the protocol's 9000, and
      9—10,000 and almost 10,000 after the capitulation;
    - Bodart: 10,000;
    - the King of Holland, 9 November: 7,000 camped under the walls;
    - Mortier, 13 November: 10,000 at Hameln and Nienburg together.
  - Hameln's guns: Höpfner's 93, 46, 21 and 15, without the unserviceable ones, make 175 (our
    arithmetic); Bodart prints 129.
  - Hameln's prisoners: about 600 (Höpfner and Bodart), against 6000 in General Savary's report as
    Höpfner gives it.
  - Savary's charge at Hameln: already in the 32nd Bulletin of 16 November; by Napoleon's order of
    18 November and announced on the 19th (Höpfner).
  - The French divisions before Hameln: Gobert and Michaud (Höpfner), Dumonceau and Michaud
    (Mortier). Mortier's brick fort on the Hamel may be Höpfner's inundation redoubt; the passages
    read do not settle it.
  - The surrender of Glogau and Breslau: Savary asserted it on the 20th, and Höpfner calls it a lie.
  - Nienburg's date: drawn up on the 25th and made known after the parole, and the place fallen
    on the 26th (Höpfner); 26 November (Bodart).
  - Nienburg's garrison: 2689 infantry, 168 artillery and 54 hussars, together 2911, in a fortress
    reckoned for 1500 (Höpfner); 3,000 (Bodart); under 1,500 (the Emperor, 22 October).
  - Nienburg's prisoners: the garrison into captivity (Bodart), against Höpfner's releases, with
    only a small remainder taken to France.
  - Nienburg's town: a bombardment would soon turn it to ashes (the council of war); its burning
    would not hinder the defence (Höpfner's description of the works).
- **Gaps.** No targeted follow-up was run.
  - At both places the most consequential gap is side A's strength. Bodart prints 6,000 for
    Savary's force at each. At Hameln it matches Höpfner's 'only 6000' and Lecoq's estimate, and
    none is shown to be a count; at Nienburg no other total is read. A return of the troops before
    Hameln for 18 to 20 November, and of Savary's column at Nienburg, could close it.
  - Deferred:
    - Höpfner pp. 351–352, 354–356, 362, 366, 368 and 374, and the untranscribed parts of
      pp. 376–378 and 383;
    - seen only in the OCR: the Hameln passages of Foucart t. II at pp. 75, 346, 749, 795 and 831,
      and a later letter of the Emperor's to the Grand Duke of Berg that repeats Nienburg's
      surrender (*Campagne de Pologne*, pp. 241–242);
    - not found in the Foucart pages read: General Savary's reports, Napoleon's orders of 13 and
      18 November, any order for the move on Nienburg, and the text of either capitulation.
  - The passages read do not show whether anyone at Hameln or Nienburg knew of the suspension of
    arms.
- **Verification.** The pages and the drafts were checked before release. The check:
  - transcribed every figure from the page images, the small ones on full-resolution crops, and
    found the OCR's year 1306 for 1806 in the date line of the Emperor's letter to Mortier of
    7 November (p. 771);
  - searched the OCR of both Foucart volumes for Hameln and Nienburg, and of *Campagne de
    Pologne* also for Savary, and read each hit in its snippet;
  - reread every cited passage against its transcription and fixed what the reread found, among it:
    - the Hameln council's protocol held that a corps of 9000 men must be dearer to the King than
      so remote a fortress; the draft no longer says the corps was "valued" so;
    - Michaud's letter allowed an officer to be sent to Blücher but then required surrender, the
      garrison becoming prisoners of war;
    - the French, not Savary in person, rejected the 5th article of Lecoq's draft;
    - Mortier's letter has the Prussian posts driven into the place, without naming who drove
      them;
    - Fort George's garrison "had many sick", as Höpfner says;
    - at Nienburg the commandant urged free withdrawal on Savary in vain; the draft no longer says
      that Savary refused it;
    - Nienburg's bridgehead had bonnets and traverses by which cover against the heights "had been
      sought";
    - a date with no figure in its claim's citations was removed from Nienburg's side-B
      information claim;
  - added passages the reread showed were needed: the Prince of Waldeck's news of the 19th,
    Mortier's statement that he could not delay his march on Hamburg, and the 10 pieces of ordnance
    he left with Dumonceau.

  The evidence check passes on all 2,857 sources and 44 drafts (880 claims, 15 unknown). The packet
  was regenerated, `make reproduce` was run, and `make check` passes (239 tests).
- **Usage.** About 1.9 million tokens for the two entries and their 91 records, by the session's
  token counter.
  - Hameln took most of it: a 32-page Prussian account, of which 23 pages were read, and 16 pages
    of French documents. Nienburg added six pages of Höpfner and two of Foucart to the documents
    it shares with Hameln.
  - The estimate for the cohort's other 281 engagements stays at about 165 to 190 million tokens.

## 1806 first pass, sub-batch 2d-i: Groß-Glogau (2026-10-18)
